@@136069515 <p> For millions of Nigerians , seeking medical care has become a test of endurance and financial survival . <p> In overcrowded public hospitals , patients wait hours for subsidised treatment , while private clinics offer faster care at costs that can wipe out a lifetime of savings . <p> advertisement <p> advertisement <p> As underfunding , staff shortages and failing infrastructure weaken the public system , a growing number of people are being pushed into seeking private care , where the price of survival is often painfully out of reach . <p> For Divine Osariemen , the crisis hit on March 24th while he was driving from Port Harcourt to Benin . A sudden darkness clouded his left eye , a terrifying realisation that grew into a frantic search for help . To confirm , he covered his right eye , only to realise he still could not see . <p> " When I got to the hospital , I discovered that I was the 60th person in the queue for the day 's consultation , " he shared with BusinessDay . <p> " After a very long wait @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ needed nine million naira for the surgery . However , I could n't wait due to delays , so I visited a private facility in Kaduna State to perform the surgery at a much higher cost , " he added . <p> advertisement <p> advertisement <p> In Lagos , Vivian Salefu faced a similar hurdle for something as routine as a hepatitis vaccine . When the public clinic told her they were out of stock , she was forced to pay N21,000 for the three doses , N7,000 each at a private facility -- a significant sum for a preventive measure that should have been accessible . <p> Nigeria 's healthcare delivery is a tale of two worlds . The public sector , built on a three-tier structure of primary , secondary , and tertiary care , boasts experienced professionals and subsidised rates . However , these halls are haunted by chronic underfunding , a " brain drain " of talent moving abroad , and infrastructure that often fails at the moment of greatest need . <p> In the vacuum left by the state , private providers have flourished , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ care . They offer shorter wait times and modern record-keeping , but these come at a price many can not pay . With over 60 percent of the population living below the poverty line , the " private option " is often a mirage . <p> " Only the citizens are left to bear the brunt , " Tuyi Mabewondu , a medical doctor , noted . <p> advertisement <p> As a result , many Nigerians are compelled to make healthcare decisions based not only on the severity of their condition but also on their financial capacity and tolerance for risk . <p> With over 60 percent of Nigerians living below the poverty line , and as much as 75 percent in rural communities per World data , experts warn that a significant share of the population remains highly vulnerable to the consequences of this widening divide . <p> Emmanuel Oziuwa-John , chief executive of Salvus Emergency , described the disparity as a structural challenge within the health system , noting that bureaucratic inefficiencies continue to slow service delivery while providers grapple with rising operational costs and limited funding . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ public and private sectors , and this is slowing down operations , shutting out communities in underserved areas while creating high operational costs for private facilities , " he said . <p> advertisement <p> Taiwo Obindo , president of the Association of Psychiatrists in Nigeria , warned that the divide is also fuelling unsafe practices . <p> " Many private healthcare facilities are now breeding grounds for quack practices , as they have taken advantage of the gap this divide has created , " he stated . <p> Observed annually on April 7 , World Health Day underscores the urgency of addressing these systemic gaps . This year 's theme , " Together for health : Stand with science , " highlights the need for stronger collaboration across healthcare systems , researchers , and both public and private stakeholders , experts say . <p> This year 's observance launches a year-long campaign celebrating the power of collaboration among researchers , private and public stakeholders to protect the health of the people , " the World Health Organisation ( WHO ) said in a press statement . <h> Public vs private @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Health , Nigeria 's healthcare delivery system operates through a dual structure where public and private providers serve overlapping populations but function in markedly different ways . <p> " The public sector is organised across three tiers . Primary healthcare is managed by local governments , secondary care is overseen by state governments , and tertiary institutions run by the federal government . In total , the system includes about 30,000 primary health centres , 4,000 secondary facilities , and around 60 tertiary institutions , " the report stated . <p> " While this network is extensive on paper , many facilities , particularly in rural areas , face chronic shortages of staff , equipment , and medicines . A significant proportion of primary health centres are either non-functional or only partially operational , especially in underserved regions , " the report stated . <p> " The private healthcare facilities have grown rapidly to fill these gaps with thousands of hospitals , clinics , and diagnostic centres , providing care to an estimated 60 to 70 percent of outpatient care , often offering more reliable services , shorter wait times @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . <p> However , coordination between the two sectors remains weak . Referral systems are inconsistent , and patient data rarely moves seamlessly across providers . This fragmentation affects continuity of care and limits the effectiveness of health data systems . While digital health initiatives are emerging to address these gaps , integration across the system is still in its early stages . <p> In addition , about 50 percent of private healthcare facilities have either shut down , downsized or charge exorbitant fees just to stay afloat due to high operational costs , which many citizens in rural or underserved communities can not afford . <p> This has created a huge gap in access , particularly for many living below the poverty line . <h> Government Efforts <p> Nigeria 's healthcare system is being reshaped through a mix of policy reforms , increased funding , and system-wide investments aimed at improving access and quality of care . The Federal Ministry of Health continues to lead national coordination , working through key agencies such as the National Primary Health Care Development Agency ( NPHCDA ) , NAFDAC , and the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ central pillar of recent reform is the Nigeria Health Sector Renewal Investment Initiative ( NHSRII ) , introduced in 2023 . The programme has mobilised over $3 billion in external funding , supported the revitalisation of more than 1,000 primary health centres , and expanded workforce capacity by retraining tens of thousands of frontline health workers . This aligns with broader efforts to strengthen primary healthcare as the foundation of the system . <p> Legislative action has also played a role . The NHIA Act 2022 made health insurance mandatory , pushing enrolment to nearly 20 million people by the end of 2024 . At the same time , federal health spending has increased significantly , rising from N1.17 trillion in 2023 to N2.71 trillion in 2025 . <p> Despite these gains , structural challenges persist . Government health spending remains below the Maputo Declaration target of 15 percent of the national budget and per capita expenditure is still low compared to regional peers . @@136125865 <h> Climate change and disaster management in Nigeria <p> Across many parts of Nigeria today , disasters no longer come as rare or unexpected events , they arrive with a disturbing sense of familiarity . From the annual floods that swallow homes and farmlands to the slow but steady advance of desertification in the north , communities are increasingly living on the edge of environmental uncertainty . What was once described as a global climate issue has now become a daily Nigerian reality , directly shaping how disasters occur and how they must be managed . <p> Climate change , driven largely by human activities such as deforestation , poor waste management , and rising carbon emissions , has intensified weather patterns across the country . Rainfall is becoming more erratic , temperatures are rising , and extreme weather events are occurring with greater frequency . In states like Kogi , Niger , and Anambra , flooding has evolved from a seasonal inconvenience into a recurring humanitarian crisis , displacing thousands and destroying livelihoods . At the same time , northern regions continue to battle desert encroachment , reducing access @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ disasters extend far beyond physical destruction . They disrupt education , weaken local economies , and stretch already limited government resources . Families are forced to abandon their homes , children are pulled out of school , and communities are left struggling to rebuild with little support . In this context , disaster management is no longer just about emergency response , it has become a critical component of national development and stability . <p> Institutions like the National Emergency Management Agency ( NEMA ) have made significant efforts in coordinating relief interventions , raising public awareness , and supporting affected communities . However , the growing intensity and frequency of climate-related disasters reveal a deeper challenge : Nigeria 's disaster management approach remains largely reactive . While responding to emergencies is essential , it is no longer sufficient in a climate-altered reality where risks are constantly evolving . <p> One of the most pressing gaps lies in the disconnect between early warning systems and community action . Although forecasts and alerts are increasingly available , many vulnerable populations either do not receive this information on time or lack the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ a more inclusive and people-centered approach , one that ensures that information is not only generated but also understood and acted upon at the grassroots level . <p> To address these challenges , Nigeria must move toward a more proactive and integrated disaster management framework . Investing in climate-resilient infrastructure is a critical starting point . Improved drainage systems , flood control mechanisms , and proper urban planning can significantly reduce the impact of flooding in high-risk areas . Equally important is enforcing environmental regulations to prevent practices that worsen climate vulnerability , such as building on floodplains or indiscriminate deforestation . <p> However , perhaps the most underutilized asset in this effort is the energy and creativity of young people . As students and digital natives , the youth are uniquely positioned to bridge the gap between information and action . Through campus-based climate awareness initiatives , social media advocacy , and community engagement , young Nigerians can serve as powerful agents of change . Establishing disaster awareness clubs in tertiary institutions , for instance , can create a network of informed individuals who not only understand climate risks @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ <p> Technology also offers significant opportunities to transform disaster management in Nigeria . Mobile platforms can be used to deliver real-time alerts , while data analytics can help predict disaster patterns and improve response strategies . When combined with community participation , these tools can create a more responsive and adaptive system that minimizes loss and enhances resilience . <p> At the policy level , consistency and accountability must be strengthened . Nigeria has developed several frameworks on climate change and disaster risk reduction , but implementation often falls short . Ensuring that policies are not only adopted but effectively enforced will be crucial in building long-term resilience . This requires coordination across all levels of government , as well as partnerships with the private sector and civil society . <p> Ultimately , climate change has fundamentally reshaped the nature of disasters in Nigeria , demanding a shift in how the country prepares for and responds to them . The solution does not lie solely in government action , but in collective responsibility . A resilient Nigeria will be built not just through policies and institutions , but through informed @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ steps to address them . <p> As the country looks towards the future , the challenge is clear : to move from a cycle of reaction to a culture of preparedness . In this transition , the role of young people can not be overlooked . With the right knowledge , tools , and opportunities , they can help redefine disaster management in Nigeria not as a response to crisis , but as a continuous effort to protect lives , livelihoods , and the environment . 
@@136125873 <p> Nigeria , Africa 's most populous country , is once again at the center of major social , economic , and political transformations . While these policies are often presented as necessary steps toward long-term economic stability , their immediate effects have been deeply felt by ordinary citizens . The cost of transportation , food , and basic goods has surged , placing immense pressure on households . @@136134671 <p> Intra-African trade is on track to reach $230 billion by 2026 , driven by the accelerated implementation of the African Continental Free Trade Area ( AfCFTA ) , with overall continental trade projected to grow 10 percent this year , according to a new report by the Afreximbank . <p> It noted that the continent 's output expanded by 4.2 percent in 2025 , up from 3.4 percent in 2024 , on the back of robust domestic demand , strong export performance , service sector growth , and renewed infrastructure investments . <p> However , Africa 's economic landscape remains vulnerable , given its overdependence on commodities , which exposes it to high prices . <p> The report showed that the continent 's total trade in 2025 reached $1.4 trillion , with intra-African trade accounting for roughly 18 percent of total trade , supported by the ongoing implementation of AfCFTA . <p> The report noted that Africa is well-positioned to enhance its economic growth prospects through a gradual recovery from recent global shocks . <p> According to the report , Africa 's GDP growth is expected to rise marginally @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ in the medium term . <p> " This recovery will be supported by increased global demand for African exports , disinflation , and the implementation of structural reforms to diversify economies across the continent , " the report said . <p> advertisement <p> advertisement <p> " Furthermore , non-resource-intensive economies are projected to outperform other groups , with their average growth accelerating sharply to about 7.5 percent in 2026 , providing further support to Africa 's growth performance in the medium term , " the report added . <p> The report assessed how African countries can capture more value from commodities to support industrialisation and trade . It reviewed commodity price volatility and global demand and the structure of Africa 's commodity trade , alongside the role of regional integration in value addition . <p> Yemi Kale , group chief economist &amp; managing director , research , African Export-Import Bank ( Afreximbank ) , in his forward note , said AfCFTA represents more than a trade agreement but an economic stabilisation mechanism in a fragmenting world . <p> " African firms , particularly small and medium-sized enterprises , remain disproportionately @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ essential not merely for trade expansion but for industrialisation and employment generation . " <p> He noted that strengthening the continent 's financial architecture is a macroeconomic priority and not a sectoral intervention . <p> He explained that the outlook report systematically traces these interconnections , evaluates global growth dynamics and monetary conditions , and assesses their spillover effects on African economies . <p> It also examines evolving trade patterns across regions and sectors and analyses financial vulnerabilities that may amplify or dampen resilience . @@136177005 <h> Gaps In Social Protection Leave Workers Vulnerable , Says ILO Report <p> A new report by the International Labour Organisation ( ILO ) has warned that millions of workers worldwide remain exposed to economic and social risks due to significant gaps in social protection systems , calling for urgent and comprehensive reforms to address the growing vulnerabilities in an increasingly volatile global economy . <p> The report , titled Universal Social Protection in Changing Labour Markets : Protecting Workers in All Types of Employment , underscores the critical need to strengthen and expand social protection frameworks to ensure that all workers , regardless of employment status , are adequately protected throughout their working lives . <p> It highlights that inadequate coverage , insufficient benefits , and weak financing mechanisms continue to undermine the effectiveness of existing systems . <p> According to the ILO , social protection systems encompass a range of policies , programmes , and employer-provided benefits designed to safeguard workers against risks such as unemployment , illness , disability , and old age . These systems also play a vital role in reducing poverty , improving overall @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the findings , Director of the ILO 's Universal Social Protection Department , Shahra Razavi , stressed that reform is no longer optional but essential . <p> She noted that countries must develop systems that are inclusive , provide adequate levels of support , and are financed in a fair and sustainable manner . Such systems , she said , are fundamental to building resilience , advancing social justice , and ensuring a fair transition in the evolving world of work . <p> The report places particular emphasis on the need to extend social protection coverage to workers who are often excluded from formal systems . <p> These include individuals in temporary and part-time roles , the self-employed , and workers in micro and small enterprises , agriculture , domestic work , and other informal or non-standard forms of employment . Expanding coverage to these groups , the ILO argues , would not only enhance social and economic outcomes but also facilitate the transition from informal to formal employment . <p> Beyond expanding access , the report stresses the importance of ensuring that benefits are both adequate and comprehensive . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ measures toward more integrated systems that provide continuous support across the life course , from early childhood and entry into the workforce to parenthood , periods of unemployment or illness , and old age . <p> Financing remains a central challenge , the report notes . The ILO advocates for sustainable funding models based on domestic resource mobilisation , including social security contributions and progressive taxation . It also highlights the role of targeted public subsidies to support workers with limited capacity to contribute . <p> For countries with constrained fiscal space , the report emphasises the continued importance of international support in building resilient and inclusive social protection systems . <p> Advertisement <p> The findings come at a time of significant global transformation driven by climate change , technological advancements , and demographic shifts . In this context , the ILO stresses that robust social protection systems are essential to helping workers and businesses adapt to change , support transitions to more sustainable economic activities , and strengthen social cohesion . <p> Overall , the report calls for coordinated and decisive action by governments , employers , and international @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ systems capable of meeting the demands of a rapidly changing labour market .

@@136277774 <p> Japa -- the outward migration of Nigeria 's young , educated workforce -- has dominated economic conversation for a decade ; an evocative shorthand for a country anxious about its future . <p> advertisement <p> But it is not Nigeria 's most dangerous demographic shift . That crisis is unfolding more quietly , far from departure halls and visa queues , in fields that are increasingly tilled by men and women in their fifties and sixties , with no one coming behind them . <p> This is the " grey exodus " : the slow ageing -- and eventual disappearance -- of Nigeria 's farmers . <p> advertisement <p> advertisement <p> The numbers , though scattered across datasets , converge on a troubling reality . The average age of a Nigerian farmer now hovers between the late forties and early fifties . In many rural communities , it is visibly higher . Travel through the rice fields of Kebbi , the cassava belts of Ogun , or the maize farms of Benue , and a pattern emerges : bent backs , greying hair , and an absence of youth @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ agriculture as a primary livelihood . Depending on methodology , youth participation in farming ranges from roughly 10% to at most a third of the agricultural workforce -- far below what demographic replacement would require . <p> In a country where over 70% of the population is under 30 , this contradiction is not just striking ; it is economically catastrophic . <p> Mechanization is minimal -- Nigeria has fewer than 0.3 tractors per 1,000 hectares , compared to over 200 in countries like the United States . Fertiliser use is similarly low , estimated at under 20 kg per hectare , far below the global average of over 130 kg . <p> advertisement <p> These are not just technical deficits . They shape the lived experience of farming -- and its attractiveness to the young . <p> Consider the economics . A smallholder farmer cultivating two hectares of maize may generate annual revenues that barely exceed subsistence once input costs , transport , and post-harvest losses are accounted for . Up to 30-40% of produce can be lost between harvest and market due to poor storage , weak logistics @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ with even modest education , the opportunity cost is clear . Farming is not merely hard work ; it is low-return , high-risk work . <p> So they leave -- not always abroad , but away from agriculture . In Lagos , Abuja , and Port Harcourt , the influx of young Nigerians into informal services , ride-hailing , retail , and digital gig work reflects this internal migration . In interviews , many express a common sentiment : farming is what their parents did because they had no choice . The result is a generational break . <p> advertisement <p> advertisement <p> In the past , agricultural knowledge , land , and labour were transmitted within families . Today , that chain is fraying . Younger family members are less willing to inherit farms , and in many cases , older farmers continue working well past retirement age simply because there is no successor . In parts of northern Nigeria , anecdotal evidence suggests that farms are being scaled down or abandoned altogether as ageing farmers can no longer manage labour-intensive cultivation . <p> This is not a distant @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ visible economic crisis : food inflation , which has surged dramatically , at times exceeding 40% year-on-year . The cost of a basic meal has risen far faster than incomes , pushing millions into food insecurity . The World Bank estimates that food price increases have been a major driver of rising poverty , with tens of millions of Nigerians unable to afford a healthy diet . <p> The CBN has raised interest rates aggressively , attempting to curb inflation through monetary tightening . Higher interest rates may dampen demand at the margins , but they do nothing to increase the supply of rice in Kebbi or tomatoes in Kaduna . We can not solve a farmer shortage with monetary policy . <p> To understand the scale of the challenge , consider Nigeria 's food import bill . Despite vast arable land -- estimated at over 70 million hectares -- Nigeria spends billions of dollars annually importing food . This dependence exposes the country to global price shocks and currency volatility , further feeding domestic inflation . <p> advertisement <p> But imports are just a symptom . The root problem @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and the demographic trajectory suggests it will produce even less in the future unless something changes . <p> There are , of course , other pressures on agricultural output . Climate change is altering rainfall patterns , increasing the frequency of droughts and floods . Insecurity in key farming regions has displaced farmers and reduced cultivated land . But even if these challenges were resolved tomorrow , the demographic problem would remain . <p> Who replaces the ageing farmer ? This is where the " grey exodus " becomes more dangerous than japa . Migration abroad , while significant , is at least partially reversible . Diaspora engagement , remittances , and return migration can mitigate its effects . But the loss of a generation of farmers is harder to reverse . Agricultural skills , land stewardship , and local knowledge are not easily rebuilt once lost . <p> advertisement <p> But there are glimpses of what a different future could look like . During the recent global cocoa price surge , parts of southwestern Nigeria experienced a mini-reversal . Young professionals , traders , and even urban workers began @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ high returns . In Ondo and Cross River , stories emerged of " weekend farmers " -- young Nigerians who split their time between city jobs and agricultural ventures . Land that had been neglected was brought back into cultivation . <p> advertisement <p> This was not driven by policy . It was driven by incentives . Where agriculture becomes profitable , young people will follow . The lesson is clear : Nigeria does not have a youth problem . It has a productivity and profitability problem . Fix those , and the demographic pipeline will begin to repair itself . <p> But this requires a shift in thinking -- from agriculture as a social sector to agriculture as a high-performance economic system . First , productivity must increase dramatically . <p> This means scaling mechanization , improving access to high-quality inputs , and deploying technology . The yield gap between Nigeria and global benchmarks is not inevitable ; it is the result of underinvestment and weak systems . <p> Second , value chains must be rebuilt . Too much of Nigeria 's agricultural output is lost or undervalued because @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . Agro-processing zones , cold storage networks , and logistics infrastructure can transform the economics of farming by reducing losses and increasing margins . <p> Third , financing must be redesigned . Traditional credit models do not work well for agriculture , particularly for young entrants without collateral . Innovative approaches -- like input credit systems -- can lower barriers to entry and enable scale . <p> Fourth , land access for young Nigerians must be addressed . Land tenure reforms , leasing models , and cooperative structures can help unlock access without requiring full ownership . But beyond all this lies a more intangible challenge : aspiration . <p> Farming in Nigeria is not currently aspirational . It is associated with hardship , uncertainty , and low status . This perception will not change through messaging alone . It will change when farming becomes visibly profitable , technologically sophisticated , and socially valued . <p> When young Nigerians see peers building wealth through agriculture -- running mechanized farms , exporting produce , leveraging technology -- the narrative will shift . Until then , the grey exodus will continue . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ not whether Nigeria can afford to act . It is whether it can afford not to . Because in the end , this is not just about agriculture . It is about sovereignty . A nation that can not feed itself is vulnerable -- economically , politically , and socially . <p> The " grey exodus " is a warning . It tells us that the current system is not reproducing itself . That a critical sector is ageing without renewal . That the future is arriving , and we are underprepared . <p> And unlike japa , this is a crisis that can not be seen in airport departure statistics , but only in the quiet absence of young hands in the fields . By the time it becomes undeniable , it may already be too late . <p> So the question remains , stark and unavoidable : who will feed Nigeria in twenty years ? If the answer is uncertain , then no amount of monetary tightening , fiscal adjustment , or exchange rate reform will be enough . Because inflation is not always a monetary phenomenon . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . <p> Dr Hani Okoroafor is a global informatics expert advising corporate boards across Europe , Africa , North America and the Middle East . @@136301409 <h> Sustainability is as African as it gets , and these women built systems to uphold it <p> Before sustainability became a global movement , these African women were reminding us of how we lived it on the continent <p> On one of my recent research stints through iconic African women in history , I came across a name that stopped me mid-scroll : Wangari Maathai . The first African woman to receive the Nobel Peace Prize , for her contributions to sustainability . Her story was so profound that it stayed with me . <p> What makes her legacy even more memorable is not only what she was able to achieve , but how ahead of her time she was . At a moment when environmental conversations were still largely technical or distant from everyday life , due to seismic cultural changes via colonialism , she was already framing sustainability as something deeply human , connected to land , dignity , survival , and community . <p> Wangari 's story led me down a path to several other African women whose contributions to sustainability shaped the continent 's environmental @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ work did not emerge from trends or terminology , but from lived urgency : deforestation , waste , energy access , and the need to build systems that could sustain both people and the planet . In many cases , these pressures were shaped by economic and colonial structures that prioritised extraction over local development , leaving gaps that had to be addressed at the community level . <h> The historical context of sustainability in Africa <p> Across Africa , sustainability has long existed in practice through systems of reuse , repair , and shared resources that prioritised longevity over excess . What changed was not the origin of these values , but their disruption . Colonial economies and global consumer culture introduced more linear patterns of consumption , gradually displacing circular , community-based ways of living with more disposable ones . <p> Over time , this influenced how value was assigned to materials , with more people gradually leaning toward disposable , linear patterns of use . Clothes that would once have been passed down or shared were increasingly discarded , and replacing items became easier than repairing or @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ entirely new , but a return that also reflects an evolution , reconnecting with long-standing ways of living while redefining them for the present . It was built through persistence , and often against resistance , by women who connected environment , community , and survival before " climate action " became a global phrase . <p> These are four of the most influential women whose work laid the foundations for environmental justice , circular systems , and sustainable development across the continent . <h> Wangari Maathai <p> Wangari Maathai via Getty Images <p> Wangari Maathai remains one of the most influential environmental thinkers to emerge from Africa.In 1977 , she founded the Green Belt Movement , a grassroots environmental organisation that mobilised rural Kenyan women to plant trees in response to deforestation , soil erosion , and declining access to essential natural resources such as firewood and clean water . <p> For Maathai , deforestation was not only an ecological issue but also tied to poverty , governance , and the disempowerment of women in rural communities . Through her initiatives , degraded lands were restored while also creating @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ a result of the movement 's activism , similar initiatives were begun in other African countries , including Tanzania , Ethiopia , and Zimbabwe . <p> She was elected to Kenya 's National Assembly in 2002 with 98% of the vote , and in 2003 she was appointed Assistant Minister of Environment , Natural Resources , and Wildlife . Then in 2004 , she became the first African woman to receive the Nobel Peace Prize , receiving global recognition for her contribution to both environmental protection and democratic rights . <p> Maathai also continued travelling the world campaigning for change . She urged action be taken on climate change , environmental justice , the protection of forests , good governance , participatory democracy , and women 's rights within Kenya . <p> As of the time of her death in 2011 , over 51 million trees had been planted through her initiative , and her legacy continues to thrive . <h> Fatima Jibrell <p> Fatima Jibrell via @goldmanprize on Instagram <p> Fatima Jibrell 's work is rooted in the environmental realities of Somalia , particularly the destruction caused by charcoal @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ environmental degradation , as well as illegal logging and fishing off the Somali coast . As a result , the natural environment was in dire need of advocates . <p> One of her most impactful campaigns targeted the charcoal trade , which was accelerating deforestation in fragile ecosystems . In 2000 , she persuaded the government to create and enforce a ban on the export of charcoal made from trees . Rather than relying on top-down enforcement , Jibrell also worked with local communities , especially women , to develop alternative livelihoods and conservation practices . She co-founded Sun Fire Cooking in 2004 as an alternative to promote the use of the butterfly-design parabolic solar cooker . <p> Her approach helped reposition sustainability in the region as something grounded in community survival and resilience , not abstract environmental policy . In 2002 , she was awarded the prestigious Goldman Environmental Prize for her work . <h> Lorna Rutto <p> Lorna Rutto via **31;1039;TOOLONG <p> Lorna Rutto is a leading voice in Africa 's circular economy movement . In 2009 , she founded Ecopost , a company that converts plastic waste @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ directly addresses two environmental challenges at once : plastic pollution and deforestation . <p> Beyond its environmental impact , Ecopost operates as a circular economy model at scale . Under Rutto 's leadership , the company has created over 300 jobs , diverting more than 1 million kilograms of plastic waste from the environment . EcoPost has also helped preserve over 250 acres of forest land , delivering both environmental protection and economic value . <p> Her work demonstrates a practical model of circular thinking where waste is not discarded , but transformed into infrastructure and long-term utility products . In 2011 , Rutto was recognised by the Cartier Women 's Initiative Awards as their laureate for sub-Saharan Africa . <p> Through her company MitiMeth , Idachaba transforms this environmental challenge into a material solution . Her organisation harvests water hyacinth and weaves it into home and personal accessories . The idea was sparked by research into similar practices in Southeast Asia , which she adapted locally by collaborating with artisans in Ibadan skilled in working with natural fibres . <p> Today , MitiMeth produces a range of handcrafted items @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ train communities across Nigeria to harvest and repurpose the plant . The model not only clears waterways but also creates new income streams , positioning waste as both a resource and an opportunity . <p> Her work has gained international recognition , including the Cartier Women 's Initiative Award , underscoring its impact at the intersection of environmental restoration and community enterprise . <h> Sustainability as lived practice , not abstraction <p> These stories show that sustainability in Africa emerges from lived conditions . Environmental challenges , limited resources , and changing livelihoods meant people had to find solutions that worked , and worked well . In response , these women built systems that responded directly to those constraints . <p> At its core , this approach is practical . It relies on local knowledge and a clear understanding of how to make things last . Items are used for longer , repurposed when possible , and built with continuity in mind . <p> Although informal , we had these systems in place , but over time , many of these ways of living started to fade . As Western @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ build and consume began to shift . Homes that once allowed for airflow , shade , and outdoor living -- often with trees and gardens -- have been replaced by more enclosed spaces that rely on artificial cooling , even when they do n't suit the climate . <p> This change also shows up in everyday habits . We prioritise trends over longevity when shopping , and things that could be repaired are replaced . Items that once stayed in circulation are treated as disposable . <p> What this moment calls for is a second look and recognition of what already worked and how those practices can shape how we live today .

@@136368071 <h> Waste to power generation option for Nigeria <p> " A clean environment is human right like any other . It is therefore part of our responsibility towards other to ensure that the world we pass on is as healthy if not healthier than we found it . " -- Dalai Lama . <p> Nigeria is fast experiencing rising growth in human population and physical development . As industrialisation and urbanisation become evident in shaping lives in municipalities , there have been global calls for adoption of effective Integrated Municipal Solid Waste Management ( ISWM ) Programmes . Nigeria , according to sources generates approximately 85,000-90,000 tonnes of solid waste per day culminating into 32m tonnes annually . The level of solid waste being generated daily is increasingly high . <p> This poses both increasing health and environmental challenges to the people . And residents in their large numbers clearly lack the basic understanding and requisite knowledge of effective solid waste management . Many do not have any idea of independent waste collectors let alone employing their services . Consequently , they freely dispose waste at unapproved or illegal dump @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ <p> This visible lack of understanding of the relevance of cleaner environment is obvious in the indiscriminate disposal of Solid Waste ( SW ) very close to residential areas , school compounds , along streets , major roads and drainages . In fact , current solid waste management practice in Nigeria relies predominantly on indiscriminate and open dumping often accompanied by burning of waste . These practices generate significant environmental challenges ; including the contamination of water ways , soil and the air , as well as infliction of environmental and health hazards . <p> ISWM is a deliberate policy of government aimed at providing clean , safe , healthy environment and promotion of living condition in a beneficial , technological , economic and sustainable manner . This concept unlocks the hidden treasures which conscious nations have turned into wealth creation , safe , and healthy-cleaner environment . However , developing nations like Nigeria do not seem to appreciate the hidden treasures of SW as environmental and living conditions continue to deteriorate . This directly contaminates the nexus between having a healthy population and flourishing national development . <p> Many @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ land mine and money spinning venture which addresses societal attitudes and behaviour , while creating employment opportunities . The practice in Nigeria almost begins and ends with agency establishments , slow enforcement functions in emergency situations or on days set aside for monthly environmental sanitation , uncoordinated waste evacuation mechanism and poor public attitude . <p> Lagos state is a shining example of how cities should address waste concerns despite observed limitations . The Lagos State Waste Management Authority ( LAWMA ) has set the standards by establishing a mechanism of integrating the law , stakeholders , technology and financing in the management of waste . Cities where successful waste management has been established begins from recorgnising the roles of the value chain -- homes , waste collectors , technology , financing , infrastructure , the law and public attitude , etc . <p> After the 2011 flooding which drastically affected over 21 states in Nigeria , both Kaduna and Oyo state governments embarked on Zaria Solid Waste Management Study/Pilot Scheme and the Ibadan Solid Waste Management Masterplan , respectively . The projects sought to address the challenges of municipal @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ in a holistic manner for the good of the states and their people . Despite state resources invested in these two projects , the expected outcomes seemed very low . Both projects could not survive political interferences thus failed to manifest its expected potentials as emphasis was more on political patronage , instead of strengthening the system and bid for sustainability . <p> The current waste management practice in most Nigeria cities neglects the great potentials of turning waste into viable agricultural resource and other economic prospects . Solid waste must be understood as what it is : having an economic value and not merely unwanted trash . The high percentage of organic matter in waste streams offers a tremendous opportunity to significantly reduce the waste going to landfill sites by converting same into compost for agriculture and electricity . <p> In Nigeria , the challenges of Solid Waste Management ( SWM ) is made worse and or even more complex with environmental crisis arising from the effect of climate change . There is also significant dearth in human capacity for administrative and technical functions as well as , poor data/information for planning . The present poor waste management practices can only translate to more environmental and health challenges as waste generation increases with rising population and changing consumption patterns in Nigeria . <p> Emmanuel Unaebgu , a renewable energy and environmental expert , in his article titled ' Poor Waste Management as Nigeria 's Bane to Achieving Sustainable Development Goals ( SDGs ) , painted a gloomy picture of how waste is unprofessionally handled in the country and its dire consequences on health and environment . According to him , " in Nigeria , over 90% of non-metallic @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ frequent as possible , these dumpsites are set on fire to reduce the volume of waste ; disrupting ambient air quality and releasing dioxins , furan , VOCs , PCBs etc. and heavy metals like mercury and lead . <p> These pollutants have long-term health consequences including cancer ; liver problems and impairment of the immune system . Furthermore , data shows that the disposal of large amounts of waste in landfills and dumpsites accounts for about 5 per cent of total global greenhouse gas ( GHG ) emissions and 12 per cent of the world 's methane ( CH4 ) emissions ; a GHG that is 23 times more potent than carbon dioxide ( CO2 ) . Energy recovery otherwise called Energy from-Waste ( EfW ) or Waste-to-Energy ( WtE ) involves the combustion of waste in a controlled chamber to produce electricity and/or heat . Energy from waste technology reduces the overall GHG emissions by as much as 80% in comparison to waste disposal in dumpsites and reduces to the barest minimum release of pollutants . What makes this projection particularly significant is not just the numbers involved but the breadth of exposure across both urban and rural settlements , showing that flooding is no longer a seasonal inconvenience but a recurring national emergency shaped by climate pressure , infrastructure gaps , and environmental mismanagement . <p> The report outlines a complex picture where risk is not isolated to riverine communities alone but extends into major cities , agricultural zones , and densely populated commercial hubs . These categories include high risk , moderate risk , and low risk zones , each representing varying degrees of vulnerability across the country . <p> The high risk category identifies 266 local government areas across 33 states plus the Federal Capital Territory , covering more than 14,118 communities . These are locations where rainfall intensity , drainage limitations , river overflow patterns , and coastal pressure combine to create a high probability of severe flooding events . The moderate risk category expands even further , covering 405 local government areas across 35 states and approximately 15,597 communities . This wide geographic spread reinforces the idea that flood risk in Nigeria is shaped by both natural climate patterns and human settlement decisions . <h> MAJOR URBAN CENTERS UNDER THREAT <p> Nigeria 's major cities represent some of the most vulnerable points in the 2026 flood outlook due to population density , infrastructure pressure , and rapid urban expansion . Lagos stands at the top of this list due to its coastal geography , low lying terrain , and high concentration of informal settlements located near waterways . Abuja also features prominently due to expanding urban construction that often outpaces drainage planning and water management systems . <p> Urban flooding in these areas is often driven by a combination of intense rainfall events and blocked drainage channels . Each type presents unique challenges and requires tailored response mechanisms . <p> Urban flooding is expected to dominate cities such as Lagos and Abuja where infrastructure development has not fully matched population growth . This type of flooding occurs when rainfall exceeds the capacity of drainage systems , often leading to rapid accumulation of water on streets , residential compounds , and commercial areas . The impact is immediate and disruptive , affecting transportation , businesses , and access to essential services . <p> Riverine flooding is expected along major river systems such as the Niger and Benue . This occurs when prolonged rainfall upstream causes rivers to overflow into surrounding floodplains . Communities located along these floodplains are particularly vulnerable , especially agricultural settlements that depend on fertile riverbanks for farming activities . <p> Coastal flooding is expected in states along the Atlantic coastline including Lagos @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ This type of flooding is driven by sea level rise , tidal surges , and storm related water intrusion . Coastal flooding often affects fishing communities , port operations , and transportation routes , making it both an economic and environmental concern . <h> DRIVERS OF INCREASING FLOOD INTENSITY <p> The 2026 flood outlook highlights several interconnected factors responsible for the rising severity of flooding across Nigeria . Climate change remains a central driver , with increasing rainfall intensity and unpredictable weather patterns contributing to sudden flood events . These changes have made traditional seasonal expectations less reliable , as rainfall can now occur in more concentrated and destructive bursts . <p> Rapid urbanisation is another major factor . As cities expand , natural drainage channels are often blocked or built over , reducing the land 's ability to absorb excess water . Informal settlements in flood prone areas further increase vulnerability , as these communities often lack proper infrastructure and are exposed to direct flood impact . <p> Poor drainage systems remain a persistent challenge in many parts of the country . Encroachment on waterways also worsens the situation , as buildings and roads constructed along natural flood paths restrict water flow and increase overflow risk . <h> GOVERNMENT 'S RESPONSE <p> The federal government through the Ministry of Water Resources and related agencies has emphasized that the 2026 flood outlook is not merely informational but a call to action . Authorities have urged state governments to integrate flood risk mapping into urban planning decisions , particularly when approving new construction projects or expanding infrastructure networks . <p> Early warning systems have been strengthened through improved data collection and digital monitoring tools that track rainfall patterns , river levels , and soil saturation . These systems are designed to provide timely alerts that can help communities evacuate or prepare before floodwaters reach critical levels . <p> State and local governments are also being encouraged to prioritize drainage rehabilitation , enforce environmental regulations , and prevent construction on floodplains . Public awareness campaigns are expected to play a role in educating citizens about preparedness measures such as clearing drainage channels , protecting property , and understanding evacuation routes @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ in Nigeria typically follows a seasonal pattern aligned with the rainy season . The 2026 outlook indicates that early rainfall effects are expected to begin between April and June , marking the onset of flash flooding in urban and semi urban areas . During this period , isolated but intense rainfall events can quickly overwhelm drainage systems . <p> The peak flooding period is expected between July and September , when sustained rainfall combines with saturated soil conditions and overflowing rivers . This is the period when riverine flooding becomes most severe and widespread displacement is most likely . Many communities located near river basins experience prolonged water coverage during this phase . <p> The decline phase is expected between October and November when rainfall gradually reduces and water levels begin to recede . However , residual flooding effects often persist in low lying areas even after rainfall decreases , delaying full recovery in affected communities . <p> Public health risks are also significant . Rural communities along riverbanks face gradual but persistent water rise that affects farmland , homes , and livestock . Urban settlements experience sudden flash floods that disrupt daily life within hours of rainfall . <p> Many of these communities lack adequate drainage infrastructure , making them highly dependent on natural water absorption systems that are increasingly overwhelmed by climate variability . In some areas , residents have developed informal coping mechanisms such as elevating homes or relocating during peak seasons , but these measures offer limited protection against severe flooding events . <p> The scale of 14,118 high risk communities highlights the widespread nature of the challenge , showing that flood preparedness must extend beyond government intervention to include local awareness , community organization , and household level planning . <h> CONCLUSION ON NATIONAL FLOOD OUTLOOK 2026 <p> Nigeria 's 2026 @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ shaped by climate change , infrastructure gaps , and human settlement patterns . Flooding is no longer an occasional natural event but a recurring national challenge that demands coordinated action . From Lagos to Kano , from coastal villages to inland river basins , the risk is shared across geography and population . The effectiveness of response in 2026 will depend not only on forecasts but on how seriously governments , communities , and individuals act on the warnings provided before the rains reach their peak .

These exercises are not merely symbolic ; they serve as practical tests of response capacity , helping to identify gaps and improve coordination before disaster strikes . <p> Yet , despite these efforts , persistent challenges remain . Early warning information does not always reach remote or underserved communities . In some cases , residents underestimate the risks or delay evacuation until it is too late . When effectively deployed , these tools can ensure that warnings are not only timely but actionable , reaching people in formats they understand and trust . <p> Ultimately , the story of flooding in Nigeria is not just about rising water levels ; it is about the intersection of environment , governance , and public behaviour . Efficiency was often secondary to capacity . Renewables will play a critical role and are already expanding rapidly . However , they are variable by nature . For industrialisation , countries also need stable , continuous power that is baseload . This is where nuclear can make a meaningful contribution . A diversified energy mix that includes renewables , nuclear , hydropower and other sources allows countries to reduce risk , improve energy security and support long-term economic growth . <p> Nuclear is not the only solution , but it is an important part of a resilient system , especially for countries with growing industrial ambitions . In this context , Rosatom is able to support countries with integrated energy solutions that combine reliability , sustainability and long-term partnership models , tailored to national development priorities . <p> How can we shift @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ ? <p> We can not rewrite history , and we should not try to . Events like Chornobyl and Fukushima shaped public perception for a reason . The starting point is respect for those concerns , not dismissal . At the same time , what is often missing in the conversation is what happened after those events . Chornobyl , in particular , fundamentally reshaped the entire philosophy of nuclear safety . It led to a complete rethinking of reactor design , emergency response , and regulatory oversight . Independent regulators were strengthened , safety responsibilities were clearly separated from operators , and safety culture became not just a principle but a legal requirement supported by continuous drills and probabilistic risk assessments . <p> Technologically , the industry also changed dramatically . Modern reactors are designed to withstand even worst-case scenarios , with multi-layered " defence-in-depth " systems , core melt traps , and passive safety mechanisms that rely on natural physical processes rather than human intervention . These are not incremental improvements . They are the direct result of lessons learned at a very high cost . But facts @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ trust through reports . They build it through experience and transparency . That is why our approach in Africa is deliberately open . <p> We create opportunities for students , young professionals and journalists to visit nuclear facilities , research centres and training programmes . When people can see how systems operate , how safety is managed , and how seriously it is taken , the conversation becomes more grounded and less abstract . There is also an important human dimension that is often overlooked . <p> The history of Chornobyl is not only a story of tragedy . It is also a story of professionalism , responsibility and the people who managed the crisis and generated the knowledge that made today 's safety standards possible . Acknowledging that the full picture helps move the discussion away from fear alone toward understanding . At the same time , we need to broaden the narrative . Nuclear is not only about power generation . It is about cancer treatment , food security , water management and high-skilled employment . But before we begin , let us see a little about the history of Earth Day ! <h> What is the origin of ' Earth Day ' ? <p> Earth Day is a movement that began on April 22 , 1970 , as a protest initiated by U.S. Senator Gaylord Nelson to advance the fight against climate change and other environmental issues and to raise awareness of environmental concerns . It began following an oil spill in Santa Barbara , California , in 1969 ; the senator , in a bid to ensure the public recognized the importance of caring for the environment , organized rallies across the U.S. , which eventually led to the creation of Earth Day . <p> The event was monumental , leading to the creation of the United States Environmental Protection Agency ( EPA ) and various environmental laws @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ U.S. into a global movement now recognized worldwide , including in Nigeria . <h> How Does Earth Day Impact Nigerians ? <p> Nigeria , like other parts of the world , continues to face the effects of climate change , which has completely rewired its weather patterns . In the past decade , Nigeria 's weather has been increasingly dominated by heat waves , which have only worsened this year . <p> The change began when the expected annual Harmattan season deteriorated and was replaced by an excessive heat wave . The country now experiences an erratic rainy season as weather conditions constantly change , with temperatures reaching alarmingly high levels . <p> The recognition of Earth Day and the importance of ensuring that we contribute to the betterment of the Earth are significant steps for Nigerians . Climate conditions can improve if we work towards sustainability . <h> How Nigerians Can Contribute to Saving the Earth <p> There are a few ways Nigerians can contribute to Earth Day 's goals . @@136546871 <h> Adaptation roadmap : When heat becomes death sentence for the poor <p> There is a quiet injustice unfolding in the world . One that does not make as much noise as war , does not command the urgency of elections , and yet may claim more lives than both . It is hot . This is not the ordinary heat Nigerians joke about seasonally , nor the familiar discomfort of a hot afternoon in Jalingo or Abakaliki . It is something more insidious : heat as a killer , intensified by climate change , and distributed along the harsh lines of global inequality . <p> A recent analysis by the Climate Impact Lab delivers a verdict that should unsettle every policymaker in Nigeria : by mid-century , about 90 per cent of deaths linked to rising temperatures will occur in low- and middle-income countries . Let that sink in . Ninety per cent -- not because these countries are naturally hotter . Not because their people are biologically weaker . But because they are poorer . Thus , it has become clear that in the age of climate change @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ selectively . <p> " This report uncovers one of climate change 's cruellest ironies -- it is projected to kill millions of people in the countries that have generally done the least to cause it . Further , their relatively low income levels mean that they are not as well-positioned as people in rich countries to confront the new and unfolding risks from climate change , " says Michael Greenstone , a co-founder of the Climate Impact Lab and the director of the Institute for Climate and Sustainable Growth and Energy Policy Institute at the University of Chicago . " In this report , we 've identified the regions around the world where climate adaptation investments can save the most lives . " <p> The findings from the new report underscore the inequality of climate change . The issue is not only that warmer regions are set to experience higher mortality than cooler ones , but also that the greatest impacts are projected to fall on regions that are both hotter and poorer , as they have fewer resources for adaptation . For example , the country of Burkina Faso @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of deaths from heat than the wealthier country of Kuwait in the Middle East , despite their similar climates . Overall , ten times more people are projected to die from the heat in poor countries than in rich countries by 2050 . <p> The report is the first in a new " Adaptation Roadmap " series that takes as its starting point the urgent need to embrace adaptation as a central part of the strategy to confront climate change , alongside greenhouse gas emissions mitigation . <p> Meanwhile , for the uninformed and the sceptic alike , there is this elementary task of stating the obvious , perennially . This is because for some citizens , it requires a leap of faith to see climate change for what it is -- a global emergency right here on our bed . Just ask your relative how this past Easter celebration was in the hinterland . It was hot as hell , even in areas that used to experience cooler Paschal atmosphere . I , for one , can testify . I travelled to my hometown in Awha , Enugu State @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ eyes . In a traditional wedding ceremony that I attended , I saw villagers pulling off their clothes as the heat became unbearable . <p> Even for those in the city , there are signs that things are no longer the same . As the years go by , what is known as the ' heat period ' grows more venomous . One would have a need to bathe due to heat , but right there in the bathroom after a cold shower , one is enveloped by another bout of heat even while still towelling . In such a moment , one gets confused by the fresh trickles of liquid dribbling down one 's back -- one 's own sweat springing from harassed pores . <p> The CIL 's projection is not prophecy . It is a warning built on data . But warnings , if ignored , become outcomes . If 90 per cent of heat-related deaths are projected to occur in countries like ours , then the real question is not whether Nigeria is at risk . It is whether Nigeria is prepared to act differently , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . In wealthy countries , when temperatures rise , people retreat indoors . Air conditioning hums : hospitals respond quickly ; urban systems absorb the shock . <p> In Nigeria , when temperatures rise , millions have nowhere to retreat to . The trader in Onitsha does not shut down because of the heat . The farmer in Sokoto can not postpone the sun . The construction worker in Abuja does not get paid for staying at home . They endure . And increasingly , some will not survive , as the parameters have been laid bare by CIL . <p> Therefore , we must as a people , extract important lessons . Nigeria combines all the risk factors highlighted by the Climate Impact Lab : A rapidly growing population , expanding cities with weak planning , heavy dependence on outdoor labour , an overstretched healthcare system , and chronic electricity shortages . <p> This is not just vulnerability ; it is structural exposure . Take our cities , Lagos , Kano , Abuja -- concrete expanding faster than trees , roofs trapping heat , power supply failing when it @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ ; they are emerging heat traps . Or consider the North , where temperatures are already pushing extremes . Add climate change , and what you have is not discomfort -- it is danger . <p> The way I see it , the coming decades will test Nigerian leadership in ways that go beyond elections and rhetoric . Shall we have leaders who build cities that do not cook their inhabitants ? This is because the question will no longer be " How many roads were built ? " or " What was the GDP growth rate ? " It will increasingly become : How many Nigerians were protected from preventable climate-linked death ? <p> That is a different standard of accountability . One that does not respond to rhetoric , but to systems : power supply , urban cooling infrastructure , health emergency readiness , labour protection laws , and environmental planning enforcement . <p> There is yet another interesting aspect . In the report , Nigeria is not on the list of the top 25 countries with the greatest increase in temperature-related mortality rates . The reason why @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ that we are blessed with diverse topography , and this contributes to the country-level average ( 8 additional deaths/100,000 ) being smaller than many of our geographic neighbours . <p> Emily Grover-Kopec , one of the authors , explained further : " There are a few different factors that help explain that diverse set of outcomes , including ( Nigeria 's ) climate and income . <p> " Climate : Nigeria 's topography contributes to more modest changes in temperature-driven mortality in some areas . Higher elevations , particularly in the central part of the country , are relatively cooler than the surrounding areas , which is associated with a more modest change in temperature-driven mortality . Our projections even call for a net decline in temperature-related mortality around Jos in the central part of the country and Sardauna in the southeast ( of Taraba State , in North-East Nigeria ) due to fewer cold-related deaths in those highland areas . <p> " This type of topography-associated behaviour is much more prevalent in Nigeria than in Ghana . Nigeria 's coastal areas are also relatively cooler than the northern 2/3 @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ described above . <p> " Income : In general , Nigeria 's current and projected future income is larger than that of many of its neighbours to the north ( northeast and northwest ) , which rank among those to be most impacted by a warming climate ( e.g. , Niger , Chad , Burkina Faso , Mali , etc . ) . We project that the largest increases in temperature-related mortality will be across Nigeria 's northern tier , bordering some of those other countries . Projected temperature changes in those areas are similar , but the relative difference in income implies that Nigeria will be better positioned to adapt to those changes than the other countries , hence the more modest projected increases in mortality . <p> " However , those regions in the northern part of the country will still see significant increases in temperature-driven mortality ( e.g. , within states of Sokoto and Borno ) . 41 of the 500 regions that we model in Nigeria will see increases in mortality that exceed Nigeria 's current death rate for HIV/AIDS , which is currently the 10th-largest @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , the report is a wake-up call . This is the time to remind the rich nations to ramp up their pledge as per the UNFCCC . The climate finance apportioned to help the poor nations fight climate change will not , in all honesty , be able to lift their misery . And for the developing nations , it is time to take adaptation strategies seriously . Nigeria , for instance , seemed never to give any thought to cooling systems for the citizens , and food preservation for agricultural products made perishable by increasing heat . Now is the time to activate adaptation strategies , even in housing policies . <p> All rights reserved . @@136556048 <p> In sub-Saharan Africa , household air pollution claims over 800,000 lives yearly , with women and children bearing the greatest brunt . Nearly 80 percent of the population still relies on wood and charcoal for cooking , making clean cooking not just an energy intervention , but also one of the most immediate and high-impact climate and public health interventions available today . <p> advertisement <p> Despite its proven benefits , the sector remains critically underfunded . The core issue is not a lack of solutions or demand , but a structural gap in financing . Without government-backed authorisation , carbon finance , the most viable mechanism for scaling clean cooking , will fail to reach its full potential . <p> Carbon markets have created a pathway to scale by monetising emissions reductions and allowing developers to significantly subsidise the cost of clean cookstoves , often by as much as 90 percent for low-income households . This model works , but only when there is trust . In today 's carbon markets , credibility is no longer defined solely by project-level verification ; it increasingly depends on whether emission @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Investors and buyers are demanding greater assurance that credits are not only real but also aligned with host country climate commitments and accounted for transparently . <p> advertisement <p> advertisement <p> This is why government authorisation has become foundational . Under Article 6 of the Paris Agreement , carbon credits intended for international use must be backed by host governments through Letters of Authorisation . These approvals elevate projects into sovereign-backed climate assets , enabling credits to be transferred as internationally transferred mitigation outcomes and used toward national or international targets . Without such authorisation , credits face limited market acceptance , higher risk premiums , and reduced investor confidence . With it , they gain legitimacy , unlock capital , and create the conditions for scale . <p> Across Africa , however , many countries remain in a transitional phase . Clean cooking projects are already delivering measurable impact , reducing emissions , protecting forests , and improving livelihoods , but they are constrained by incomplete regulatory frameworks and unclear authorisation processes . This creates a bottleneck where projects are ready to scale , but financing can not flow @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ a missed opportunity for both climate action and economic development . <p> Nigeria is beginning to demonstrate what a different trajectory can look like . At a presentation during the second meeting of the National Council on Climate Change , Omotenioye Majekodunmi , director general , noted that the country is now positioned to tap into new rounds of multilateral climate finance . <p> She outlined three critical priorities : adopting a national carbon market framework capable of unlocking up to $3 billion annually , operationalising the climate change fund to enable immediate mobilisation of resources , and restoring the NCCC budget line within the annual FAAC allocation to ensure long-term financial stability . These steps signal a deliberate shift toward building a functional and investable carbon market ecosystem . <p> advertisement <p> This direction is already taking shape . Nigeria 's Carbon Market Activation Policy and the development of a national carbon registry point to a shift toward structured governance and stronger oversight . The country is positioning itself not just as a passive host of projects but as an active regulator of carbon integrity . <p> In a @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Authorisation to approved project developers in March , marking a shift from policy intent to market implementation . Early projects that received authorisation , including BURN , a major clean cookstove manufacturer , illustrate how this framework can translate into tangible outcomes . <p> Authorisation paves the way to carbon financing that enables local manufacturing , job creation , and the large-scale distribution of clean cooking solutions through facilities such as BURN 's ISO-certified plant in Kano . These are not isolated corporate efforts but emerging examples of how a functioning carbon market can anchor domestic value chains while delivering climate and development benefits . <p> advertisement <p> advertisement <p> The authorisation of such projects signals to global markets that Nigeria is not only open for high-integrity carbon trading under Article 6 but is also building a system where private sector participation is aligned with national priorities and long-term sustainability goals . <p> If clean cooking is to scale across Africa , this kind of policy clarity must become the norm rather than the exception . Governments need to move decisively to operationalise Article 6 frameworks , establish transparent and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ national climate strategies . <p> The opportunity is significant : carbon finance has the potential to channel billions of dollars into African economies , supporting local manufacturing , creating jobs , and accelerating access to modern energy . But this will only happen where regulatory certainty exists . Governments can accelerate this by establishing clear guidelines and standards for carbon credits , ensuring transparency and accountability , and embedding carbon markets firmly within their Nationally Determined Contributions . <p> The technology is available , the demand is undeniable , and the financing model has been proven . What remains missing in many markets is the enabling policy environment . Without government-backed authorisation , clean cooking carbon projects will remain fragmented and underfunded . With it , they can scale rapidly , reaching millions of households and delivering one of the fastest and most tangible climate wins available today . <p> advertisement <p> Josephine Okojie-Okeiyi is an Assistant Editor with BusinessDay . She reports on industry , trade , agriculture , climate change , environmental issues and entrepreneurship . <p> Josephine Okojie-Okeiyi is a journalist with over five years ' reporting @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , climate change , and environmental issues . She is fellow of Thomson Reuters Foundation and Bloomberg Media Initiative for Africa . <p> Concerned members of the Kwara State Chapter of the All Progressives Congress ( APC ) have written to President Bola Tinubu , raising ... <p> 1 Min Read <p> Business Day , established in 2001 , is a daily business newspaper based in Lagos . It is the only Nigerian newspaper with a bureau in Accra , Ghana . It has both daily and Sunday titles . It circulates in Nigeria and Ghana .

@@136562261 <p> Speaking at the 122nd inaugural lecture at the University of Jos , titled " Understanding Droughts : A Panacea for National Food Security , " Binbol said the frequency of drought in Nigeria has risen steadily over the years . He attributed this trend to climate variability , increasing temperatures , and human activities such as deforestation and carbon emissions . <p> He explained that drought does not occur suddenly but unfolds in stages . It often begins as meteorological drought caused by insufficient rainfall . If the situation persists , it develops into agricultural drought that affects crops , followed by hydrological drought impacting rivers and groundwater , and eventually socio-economic drought , which disrupts livelihoods and economic productivity . <p> " Drought is a silent phenomenon . Unlike floods or earthquakes , its impact is not immediately visible , but it ranks first among natural hazards in terms of long-term impact . Its effects are cumulative , spread over large geographical areas , and can severely affect food production and water availability , " he said . <p> Binbol noted that regions north of latitude 9 ? @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . According to him , rainfall generally declines with increasing latitude due to the shifting position of the Inter-Tropical Discontinuity . <p> He also warned that climate projections point to rising temperatures on the Jos Plateau , a development that could accelerate evaporation , deplete soil moisture , and ultimately reduce crop yields . <p> " Climate change is expected to increase both the frequency and severity of drought . When rainfall falls below long-term averages and temperatures keep rising , we begin to see reduced crop yields , declining livestock productivity , and mounting pressure on water resources , " he added . <p> The professor cautioned that prolonged drought could trigger a range of challenges , including food shortages , reduced hydropower generation , higher irrigation costs , and even conflicts over limited water resources . He added that such pressures may also drive increased rural-to-urban migration . <p> He further revealed that hydrological studies show declining water tables in parts of Plateau State , forcing many communities to dig deeper wells to access water . <p> Also speaking at the event , the Vice-Chancellor of the University @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ timely , stressing the urgency of tackling food security amid rapid population growth . <p> " The world is not expanding , but our population is . We must find ways of utilising our land and resources efficiently to ensure that we produce enough food to feed our growing population , " he said . <p> Ishaya warned that unsustainable practices such as deforestation and poor resource management continue to threaten environmental stability and food production . He called for stronger , research-driven policies to confront the challenges posed by climate change . <p> He added that institutions like the National Institute for Policy and Strategic Studies have a vital role to play in translating academic research into practical policy frameworks . <p> In his remarks , the Director-General of the institute , Ayo Omotayo , said the lecture highlighted the close link between climate understanding and food production , warning that inaction could deepen food insecurity . <p> " It is clear that if we do not understand the climate very well , we will certainly have problems with food production . @@136612903 <h> Delta community where residents drink human waste-polluted river <p> Torugbene , in Burutu Local Government Area of Delta State , is a remote riverine community where residents depend on a river contaminated with human waste for their daily water needs , exposing them to serious health risks . " <p> Her grief is not an isolated case . <p> In the same Torugbene community , 35-year-old Vivian Kiji is also mourning her one-year-old daughter , Peace , who died on April 8 , 2026 . <p> According to the infant 's mother , her body began to swell , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ body was swelling .
